I. 
Introduction 
1 
Q. 
Please identify the members of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations 
2 
Panel (“GIOP”). 
3 
A. 
The Panel consists of Ross W. Turrini, Timothy S. Graham, Caroline Hon, and 
4 
Tatiana Roc.   
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Is this the same Panel that previously submitted direct testimony on behalf 
7 
of Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“Niagara 
8 
Mohawk” or the “Company”) in these proceedings?  
9 
A. 
Yes.  The defined terms in the Panel’s direct testimony have the same meanings 
10 
in this corrections and updates testimony.     
11 
 
12 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the Panel’s corrections and updates testimony? 
13 
A. 
The purpose of the Panel’s testimony is to identify and explain the following 
14 
corrections and updates: 
15 
• Updates to the Capital Plan – The Panel identifies the following updates 
16 
and corrections to the Company’s gas capital programs and projects:  (i) a 
17 
further deferral of the Albany Loop Project that will decrease costs 
18 
between FY 2021 – FY 2025; (ii) updates to two capital projects in the Gas 
19 
System Reinforcement Program – Rexford and Altamont – to reflect 
20 
revisions to the cost estimates and/or timelines for the projects; and (iii) 
21 


--- Page 4 ---
Corrections and Updates Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 2 of 10 
 
updates to the forecast for the proposed Operator Qualification (“OQ”) 
1 
White Paper Implementation program to reflect the Company’s revised 
2 
approach to conducting performance evaluations.    
3 
 
4 
• Updates to the O&M Forecast – The Panel updates its O&M forecast to 
5 
reflect (i) an increase in costs in Data Year 1 and Data Year 2 associated 
6 
with the IVP program and (ii) corrections to the Company’s allocation of 
7 
labor expense and start dates associated with certain incremental FTEs 
8 
proposed in the direct testimony of the Gas Safety Panel.   
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Is the Panel sponsoring any exhibits as part of its corrections and updates 
11 
filing? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  The Panel sponsors the following exhibits that were prepared by one or 
13 
more members of the Panel or under their supervision and direction: 
14 
• Exhibit ___ (GIOP-1CU): Revised Actual and Projected Direct Capital 
15 
Expenditures: Historic Test Year and April 1, 2020 – March 31, 2025 (FY 
16 
2021 – FY 2025)  
17 
• Exhibit __ (GIOP-5CU): Incremental O&M Non-Labor Expenditures: 
18 
Rate Year, Data Year 1, and Data Year 2 
19 
• Exhibit ___ (GIOP-6CU): Incremental Full Time Equivalent Positions by 
20 
Function in the Rate Year, Data Year 1, and Data Year 2

2

3

4 
 
5 
II. 
Corrections and Updates 
6 
A. Updates to the Capital Plan 
7 
Q. 
Please summarize the updates to the Company’s capital investment plan 
8 
and Exhibit __ (GIOP-1). 
9 
A. 
The Company’s capital plan has been updated to reflect scheduling and/or 
10 
revised forecasts in the Rate Year and Data Years for the following capital line 
11 
items:  (i) the Albany Loop Project; (ii) the Rexford and Altamont 
12 
Reinforcement Projects; and (iii) OQ White Paper Implementation.  The 
13 
revised capital forecast is provided as Exhibit __ (GIOP-1CU), which 
14 
highlights the changes from the plan included with the Panel’s direct 
15 
testimony.  The updates to each program are discussed in more detail below. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
What is the impact of these changes to the Company’s forecast capital 
18 
expenditures in the Rate Year and Data Years? 
19 
A. 
As shown in Table 1 below, the Company has updated its capital forecast to 
20 
reflect a net decrease of $0.900 million in FY 2021, a net increase in FY 2022 
21 


--- Page 6 ---
Corrections and Updates Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 4 of 10 
 
of approximately $9.053 million, a net increase in FY 2023 of approximately 
1 
$6.714 million, a net decrease in FY 2024 of approximately $36.607 million, 
2 
and a net increase in FY 2025 of $4.173 million.  These figures are inclusive 
3 
of cost of removal (“COR”). 
4 
 
5

The cumulative impact of these changes over this period is a $17.567 million 
9

10 
corrections and updates testimony addresses the revenue requirement impact of 
11 
the Company’s revised capital forecast.   
12 
 
13 
 
i. 
Albany Loop Project 
14 
Q. 
Please describe the changes to the Albany Loop Project. 
15 
A. 
The Company is further deferring project development, construction, and the 
16 
in-service date for the Albany Loop Project by one year.  As discussed in the 
17 
Company’s response to Information Request No. DPS-754, no design-day 
18 
supply shortfalls are forecast to occur over the ten-year forecast ending with the 
19 


--- Page 7 ---
Corrections and Updates Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 5 of 10 
 
winter of 2029-2030.  Based on the current demand forecast filed in this case, 
1 
however, a design-hour shortfall is projected in the Albany area beginning in 
2 
the winter of 2027-2028.  Deferral of the project by one year provides an 
3 
opportunity to further evaluate the future impact of the portfolio of demand side 
4 
management solutions the Company is developing as potential non-pipes 
5 
alternatives to this project.  While the Company believes that the reliability 
6 
benefits of this project remain important to the customers in the areas served, 
7 
alternatives that could potentially help address the reliability concerns will be 
8 
investigated as part of the demand side management portfolio development 
9 
effort. 
10 
 
11 
As shown in Exhibit __ (GIOP-1CU), other than minor increases in FY 2021 
12 
and FY 2022, the project costs included in the forecast have shifted forward one 
13 
year to reflect the project’s deferral.  The overall result of deferring the Albany 
14 
Loop Project is a $34.20 million reduction in the Company’s capital forecast 
15 
between FY 2021 and FY 2025.   
16 
 
17 
 
ii. 
Updates to Reinforcement Projects 
18 
Q. 
Please briefly explain the Rexford and Altamont Reinforcement Projects. 
19 
A. 
As explained in this Panel’s direct testimony, the Company’s Gas System 
20 
Reinforcement Program contains several projects intended to maintain 
21 


--- Page 8 ---
Corrections and Updates Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 6 of 10 
 
minimum pressures on the gas network during peak demand periods.  The 
1 
Rexford and Altamont Reinforcement Projects were included in the Gas System 
2 
Reinforcement Program to address below minimum pressure concerns and 
3 
improve reliability on their respective systems. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Please describe the changes to the Rexford Reinforcement Project. 
6 
A. 
This Company’s capital forecast included $2.30 million, $6.00 million, and 
7 
$0.070 million for the Rexford Reinforcement Project in FY 2021 – FY 2023, 
8 
respectively, based on a preliminary estimate for the project.  The project has 
9 
since been re-estimated based on the final design.  The re-estimate increased 
10 
the FY 2021 and FY 2022 project costs by approximately $0.200 million and 
11 
$9.464 million, respectively, for a total project cost of approximately $18.034 
12 
million.  The increase in the project’s costs is attributable to increased costs 
13 
associated with working on state roads in Upstate New York (e.g., obtaining 
14 
permits, easements, restoration stipulations) as well as constructing a new 
15 
regulator station. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Please describe the changes to the Altamont Reinforcement Project. 
18 
A. 
The Company included $0.500 million in its capital forecast for FY 2021 for 
19 
the last phase of the Altamont project, as most of the construction, and the 
20 
associated costs, were originally scheduled for FY 2020.  The project was 
21 


--- Page 9 ---
Corrections and Updates Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 7 of 10 
 
recently 
redesigned 
to 
accommodate 
scope 
changes 
and 
address 
1 
constructability concerns associated with the project’s location.  The redesign 
2 
resulted in a shift in the construction plan from FY 2020 to FY 2023.  The shift 
3 
in construction plan resulted in a $0.500 million decrease in FY 2021 and 
4 
increased the project’s forecast cost in this case by $0.200 million, $10.75 
5 
million, and $0.200 million in FY 2022, FY 2023, and FY 2024, respectively. 
6 
 
7 
iii. 
OQ White Paper Implementation 
8 
Q. 
Please describe the changes to the OQ White Paper Implementation 
9 
expenditures. 
10 
A. 
It was recently decided that the Company’s Learning and Development 
11 
(“L&D”) group will retain responsibility for OQ performance evaluations.  The 
12 
L&D group is well-positioned to continuing performing this function efficiently 
13 
and can deliver the training in a more cost-effective manner than creating a 
14 
separate group.  This change results in a reduction of approximately $3.679 
15 
million from the FY 2021 – FY 2025 capital forecast, as reflected in Exhibit __ 
16 
(GIOP-1CU).   
17 
 
18 
B. Updates to O&M Forecasts 
19 
Q. 
Please describe the updates to the O&M forecasts. 
20 


--- Page 10 ---
Corrections and Updates Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 8 of 10 
 
A. 
The corrections and updates to the Company’s O&M forecast are shown in 
1 
Exhibit __ (RRP-3CU), Schedule 27 and are summarized as follows: 
2 
• IVP Program:  The Company’s forecast intended to reflect a ten percent 
3 
reduction in FY 2022.  The Company, however, inadvertently applied a ten 
4 
percent program reduction to the program costs for all three rate years (FY 
5 
2022 – FY 2024).  The Company corrected this error in the O&M forecasts 
6 
for FY 2023 and FY 2024. 
7 
• Incremental FTEs:  Updates have been made to the labor expense allocated 
8 
for and start dates of certain incremental FTEs requested in the direct 
9 
testimony of the Gas Safety Panel.  Specifically, the Company corrected the 
10 
allocations for the (i) one FTE requested for Pipeline Safety Management – 
11 
Stakeholder Engagement, (ii) two FTEs requested for the Enhanced First 
12 
Responder Train-the-Trainer Program, (iii) 16 FTEs requested for Service 
13 
Regulator Inspection, and (iv) two FTEs requested for High Emitter Leak 
14 
Repair.  The Company inadvertently allocated 100 percent of labor expense 
15 
for the Stakeholder Engagement FTE to Niagara Mohawk rather than 19.94 
16 
percent, as indicated in the Company’s response to Information Request No. 
17 
DPS-732.  Correction of the allocation results in a $0.063 million decrease 
18 
in operating expense in the Rate Year.  With respect to the FTEs for the 
19 
First Responder Train-the-Trainer Program, originally the costs were 100 
20 
percent allocated to Niagara Mohawk gas.  As discussed in the Company’s 
21 


--- Page 11 ---
Corrections and Updates Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 9 of 10 
 
response to Information Request No. DPS-831, however, because the 
1 
program will provide services that will support both electric and gas first 
2 
responder programs, the Company has revised the allocation to the common 
3 
allocator (83 percent electric/17 percent gas) in the revenue requirement.  
4 
This results in an equal and offsetting $0.194 million operating expense 
5 
increase for Niagara Mohawk electric and decrease for Niagara Mohawk 
6 
gas in the Rate Year.  With respect to FTEs for the Service Regulator 
7 
Inspection and the High Emitter Leak Repair programs, the Company 
8 
inadvertently switched the capital and expense allocation rates of these 
9 
programs.  Correction of the allocations results in a $0.317 million increase 
10 
in operating expense in the Rate Year.  This correction was noted in the 
11 
Company’s response to Information Request No. DPS-474.  Additionally, 
12 
updates to the start dates of certain FTEs associated with the Capital 
13 
Investment Plan support were made to reflect a revised workplan.  The 
14 
updates result in a $0.034 million decrease in operating expense in the Rate 
15 
Year and were identified in the Company’s response to Information Request 
16 
No. DPS-473. 
17 
 
18 
These changes are reflected in Exhibit__(RRP-11CU) Workpapers to 
19 
Exhibit (RRP-3CU), Schedule 27, Workpaper 2.  As these changes are 
20 


--- Page 12 ---
Corrections and Updates Testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and Operations Panel 
 
Page 10 of 10 
 
purely for expense allocation, no revisions to the descriptions of the 
1 
positions in the Gas Safety Panel direct testimony are required. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Are any other revisions to the GIOP exhibits required? 
4 
A. 
Yes.  In the Gas Safety Panel direct testimony, the Company requested 26 
5 
incremental FTEs – 21 field inspectors and 5 supervisors – for the Enhanced 
6 
Contractor Safety Inspections Program.  Exhibit __ (GIOP-6) inadvertently 
7 
transposed the total number of FTEs requested in these two categories.  Exhibit 
8 
__ (GIOP-6CU) has been revised to reflect the correct number of inspectors and 
9 
supervisors requested. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Does this conclude your testimony? 
12 
A. 
Yes, it does.
13

Request: 
 
 
Note: 
In all interrogatories, all requests for workpapers or supporting calculations shall 
be construed as requesting any Word, Excel or other computer spreadsheet 
models in original electronic format with all formulae intact and unlocked. 
 
Referring to Exhibit___(RRP-11), Workpapers to Exhibit RRP-3, Schedule 27, Workpaper 2, as 
it should be updated to include the missing positions as requested in DPS-472, the Company 
plans on hiring additional full-time equivalent (FTE) employees by the end of the Rate Year.   
 
1. 
For each FTE, if the anticipated start date has already passed, state if the position is 
currently filled, and whether the position was filled internally or externally. 
 
2. 
For each of the positions that were filled, state the actual start date and the amount of 
days between the actual start date and the anticipated start date presented in the 
Company’s workpapers. 
 
3. 
For each position that has not been filled and the anticipated start date of the position has 
passed, explain why the position has not been filled. 
 
4. 
For any positions that have been hired internally, explain whether the Company has 
backfilled the vacated position. 
 
5. 
Provide monthly updates to Questions 1 through 4, above on an on-going basis. 
 
Response:  
 
1. 
See Attachment 1 for the information requested in Parts 1 through 4.  The additional 
full-time equivalents (FTE) for Legal and the EAP program and the removal of one 
RNG procurement program FTE, as discussed in the Company’s response to DPS-472, 
are reflected in Attachment 1. A column titled “Corrections and updates to original

NMPC Req. No. NM-511 
NIAGARA MOHAWK POWER CORPORATION d/b/a NATIONAL GRID 
Case Nos. 20-E-0380 and 20-G-0381 
Electric and Gas Rates 
Request for Information 
FROM: 
DPS Staff, Sarah Keymel 
TO: 
 
National Grid, Revenue Requirements Panel 
SUBJECT:   Other Initiatives - FTEs 
Request: 
Note:
In all interrogatories, all requests for workpapers or supporting calculations shall 
be construed as requesting any Word, Excel or other computer spreadsheet 
models in original electronic format with all formulae intact and unlocked. 
Referring to Exhibit___(RRP-11), Workpapers to Exhibit RRP-3, Schedule 27, Workpaper 2, as 
it should be updated to include the missing positions as requested in DPS-472, and the Excel 
workpapers these figures are derived from: 
1.
For each of the incremental positions NMPC plans to fill by the end of the Rate Year, that 
are to be fully charged to NMPC, explain how the Company determined 100% of the 
position’s time should be charged to NMPC.  Provide all supporting documentation and 
calculations.   
2.
For each of the incremental positions NMPC plans to fill by the end of the Rate Year, that 
are to be partially charged to NMPC, explain how the Company determined the percent 
of a position’s time to be charged NMPC.  Provide all supporting documentation and 
calculations. 
3.
For each incremental position NMPC plans to fill by the end of the Rate Year, explain 
how the Company determined the percent of time charged to electric versus gas.  
4.
For each incremental position NMPC plans to fill by the end of the Rate Year, explain 
how the Company determined the percent of time charged to operating expense. 
5.
For each position for which the Company lists a low salary and an average salary, explain 
what these salaries represent and how they were derived. 
6.
Explain the Company’s reasoning for using the low to average midpoint salary for each 
position. 
Appendix A 
Page 7 of 33


--- Page 32 ---
Form 103  
7.
For each incremental position NMPC plans to fill by the end of the Rate Year, identify 
whether the position is an entry level position, and how the Company made that 
determination. 
8.
For each incremental position NMPC plans to fill by the end of the Rate Year, identify 
whether the position is expected to be filled internally or externally, and how the 
Company made that determination. 
Response:  
1.
See Attachment 1 for the information requested in Parts 1 through 4 and Parts 7 and 8.  
The additional full-time equivalents (“FTE”) for legal and the EAP program, the 
removal of one RNG procurement program FTE, and the allocation rates changes for the 
pipeline safety stakeholder engagement FTE, the multi-use hydrogen FTE, and the first 
responder training FTEs, as discussed in the Company’s response to DPS-472, are 
reflected in Attachment 1.  A column titled “Corrections and Updates to Original Filing” 
in Attachment 1 indicates these and other changes that the Company will reflect in its 
corrections and updates filing.   
2.
See response to Part 1. 
3.
See response to Part 1. 
4.
See response to Part 1. 
5.
The salaries in the referenced Workpaper 2 are from payroll as of December 31, 2019, 
the end of the Historic Test Year.  Payroll for the operating company to which the FTE 
is assigned is used to determine the salary for the FTE’s job title.  If payroll for that 
operating company does not have the job title, then the job title in Service Company 
payroll is used to determine the salary for the job title.  The final salary used in 
calculating the cost associated with the FTE is the midpoint between the lowest and 
average salary on payroll for the job title. 
6.
FTE positions can be filled by candidates with varying levels of experience and thus at 
varying levels of starting salary.  Using the mid-point between the lowest salary on 
payroll and average salary on payroll is a conservative approach to determine a salary 
that accounts for this variability.  Also, this method of determining salary has been 
consistently used and accepted by Staff in National Grid’s previous four New York rate 
cases. 
7.
See response to Part 1.

8.
See response to Part 1.

Transmission Control Center System           1.0 
           1.0 
           1.0 Management
100%
96.0%
4.0%
0.0%
0.0%
All incremental positions requested are in support of 
the New York Control Center Operations. The FTE’s 
will be reporting into the NY Transmission and NY 
Regional Control Centers to support day to day 
operations. The FTE’s will not be performing work 
for other jurisdictions.
All incremental FTE’s are in support of the Electric Control Center Operations and
The incremental positions operating expense 
percentage was based on recent past trends of 
existing like positions, their duties and 
responsibilities. Future allocations to operating 
expense are projected to be similar.
These positions are not entry level position and require several years of 
operational experience in the electric industry. All of the Control Center 
Operations positions will require skills associated with switching and 
clearance and control that impact the health and safety of workers and 
system reliability.
The positions may be filled internally or externally dependent on 
qualications of the internal candidate pool. If qualified internal 
candidates cannot be found the search will continue outside of 
the Company. The qualifications will include education and years 
of related experience.

0.0%
Employee will support UNY Electric and will charge 
100% OPEX
This is an electric only role that will exclusively support emergency planning for 
the electric function
This is a 100% OPEX Function 
 This role requires an established set of core credentials and related 
experience. Depending on experience - this could be either an entry level 
program managers role or not. 
The expectaion would be to fill the position internally. A working 
knowledge of Company process, procedure, and emergency 
resposne is desireable and will shorten the learning curve 
associated with becoming proficient in the role.

Grid Mod
Distribtuion Planning Engineer
           1.0 
           1.0 
           1.0 Management
100%
30.0%
70.0%
0.0%
0.0%
The 100% allocation to NMPC was determined for 
this FTE due to all work assigned being in the New 
York jurisdiction of the Company.
This position is for an Electric Distribution Planning Engineer. The scope of 
work for this position will be strictly Electric with no focus on the Gas 
business.
The Incremental positions O&M Forecast was 
determined by looking at past trends of existing 
similar positions, their responsibilities and 
assignments. Future allocations to O&M are 
projected to be similar. 
 Based on the qualifications of the candiates that apply to this position, it 
could be an entry level position or higher if the proper candidate is found.  
These positions may be filled internally or externally depending 
on the candidate pool and the applicants qualifications.

332 EIOP
Capex Growth
Distribution Designer
           1.0 
           1.0 
           1.0 Union
100%
40.0%
60.0%
0.0%
0.0%
This role at National Grid resides in Upstate NY and 
works only on projects in the traditional Niagara 
Mohawk area.  They do not design or engineer for 
any other of the territories. 
This is an electric only position in the NMPC territory, therefore will only be 
charged to NMPC electric.
The Distribution Design department has a long 
history of designing capital projects, this percentage 
split is based on historical departmental data.
These positions are entry level, being that they are in addition to existing 
staff the positions they will be posted at the lowest level of the job 
progression series.  The majority of recent new hires in Design have been 
hired out of college and new to the industry.
Being Union positions they are posted internally first, then if no 
qualified bidders are internal to the company the position will be 
posted externally.  The vast majority of the Design Rep positions 
are filled with external candidates, I predict all of these will be 
external fills.

These positions are supporting outdoor lighting and 
related activities driven by municipalities within 
NMPC territory.
These positions will be supporting outdoor lighting and related activities, none of 
which include gas services.
The Company reviewed the various tasks these 
positions are expected to perform, categorized them 
as Opex or Capital, and estimated the number of 
hours it would take to perform each task on an 
annual basis.  This result is being used to estimate 
the % of time that will be charged to Opex.
 These are entry level Consumer Rep (union) positions. Successful 
candidates will be selected from a bid list and trained to be able to perform 
requisite tasks.  
In accordance with union protocol, the Company will first 
endeavor to fill the position internally.  To the extent it is unable 
to find a qualified candidate through the bidding process, the 
Company will need to post the position externally.

6. 
Will the requested FTE position have job duties other than those needed to address PSMS 
stakeholder engagement?  If so, describe the duties. 
Response:  
1a.   
The FTE will provide services to Niagara Mohawk Corporation (“Niagara Mohawk”), The 
Brooklyn Union Gas Company (“KEDNY”), and KeySpan Gas East Corporation 
(“KEDLI”).   
1b. 
The position will be allocated proportionally based on the number of gas customers served 
by each New York operating company.  In the Company’s accounting system, this 
allocation is defined by allocation code C207, which has a 19.94% allocation to Niagara 
Mohawk.  The revenue requirement in the Company’s initial filing incorrectly reflects an 
allocation of 100% to Niagara Mohawk per Exhibit___(RRP-3), Schedule 27.  This 
allocation will be revised to 19.94% in the Company’s Corrections and Updates filing.  
1c. 
The federal and state gas safety regulations apply to all New York operating companies.  
A dedicated FTE will be better positioned to provide consistent review, oversight, and 
delivery of programs and projects to support the Company’s performance in this area.  This 
will ensure that necessary controls are put in place and applied to these activities, which 
will increase the likelihood of program success for all operating companies.  Accordingly, 
successful implementation of programs provides pipeline safety benefits for all customers, 
including Niagara Mohawk’s customers. 
2.   
The job duties of the requested FTE position include: 

Manage Niagara Mohawk and KEDNY/KEDLI Residential Methane Detector (RMD) 
Pilots and Programs, including regulatory reporting; 

Manage monthly trackers to manage gas safety programs adopted in rate cases; 

Satisfy Regulatory Quarterly and Annual Reporting Requirements for the gas safety 
projects and programs managed; 

Manage programs adopted in response to proceedings related to Pipeline Safety and 
Public Awareness;  

Manage regulatory requirements of API RP1162 and API RP1173 specific to Pipeline 
Safety Public Awareness and Stakeholder Engagement;  

Prepare for, and respond to, Public Awareness Program Audits for all New York 
operating companies;  

Coordinate annual internal review of National Grid Public Awareness Policy 
(#GEN02010) to ensure that the program has been: 
o
Developed and implemented according to the guidelines of API RP1162; 
o
Developed and written to address the objectives, elements, and baseline schedule 
of API RP1162; and 
o
Implemented and documented according to the written program. 
3. 
Currently, the Company uses various members of the existing Stakeholder Engagement 
team to cover these regulatory program management tasks.  This approach leads to uneven 
delivery of program outcomes with potentially negative impacts on other Public Awareness 
Appendix A

Form 103  
and stakeholder engagement activities.  The Company believes that consolidating the 
responsibilities into one role will aid with consistency and result in more positive program 
outcomes and activities.   
4.   
No. 
5.   
The Company identified the need because of the increasing demand and strain on the 
Stakeholder Engagement team to manage numerous regulatory program mandates.  These 
mandates include, but are not limited to: 








Pipeline Safety Stakeholder Engagement activities and programs adopted in regulatory 
proceedings.

The FTE’s primary responsibilities will focus on PSMS Stakeholder Engagement, 
including continued focus on Pipeline Safety Engagement with our key stakeholders, First 
Responders, Public Officials, Excavators & Affected Public.  From time to time, the FTE 
may also provide support to the Damage Prevention organization to aid in addressing third 
party and no-call damages, align with One-Call Centers, and engage with Public Officials.

FROM: 
DPS Staff, Davide Maioriello

SUBJECT:   Albany Loop Project 
Request: 
Note:
In all interrogatories, all requests for workpapers or supporting calculations shall

On page 75 of the Direct Testimony of the GIOP, the Company describes temporarily deferring 
the Albany Loop Project: 
1. 
Has the Company solicited for any non-pipe alternatives (NPA) to the project?  If so, 
provide details of such solicitations and outcome.  If not, describe in detail why the 
Company has decided not to do such solicitations for NPAs. 
2. 
If the Albany Loop Project does not receive the necessary approvals to commence 
implementation of the project, what are the expected system reliability and supply 
impacts that such a determination would have to the service areas?  Provide a service area 
location and time frame for all listed impacts including any expected moratoria on gas 
service. 
3. 
Provide a separate long-term 10 to 15-year supply/demand forecast for the design-day.  
Include a list of all major capital projects with projected in-service dates the Company 
plans to implement to meet the need of the forecast demand. 
4. 
Provide a 10-year peak-hour (design-hour) supply demand chart for East Gate. 
Response:  
1.
The proposed Albany Loop Project is designed to provide significant customer benefits, 
including distribution system resiliency for approximately 50,000 gas customers by 
allowing gas supplies to flow to these customers from multiple sources in the event of a 
Appendix A 
Page 18 of 33


--- Page 43 ---
Form 103  
failure.  The project also mitigates projected hourly and daily contractual shortfalls at 
several city gate interconnects with Dominion Energy Transmission, Inc. (“DETI”). 
The Company has not considered any specific non-pipe alternatives (“NPAs”) for this 
project because there is no single NPA that could provide the same level of operational 
benefits.  The Company, however, is developing a portfolio of demand side management 
solutions, as well as a process for soliciting and reviewing potential NPAs.  As described 
in the Company’s testimony, the cumulative impact of these demand side initiatives could 
defer the need for the Albany Loop Project.  Please see the Company’s response to 
PACE_AGREE-077 for a description of the Company’s general approach to reviewing 
NPAs. 
2. 
The reliability and supply impacts are described in the Company’s response to Question 1 
above. 
No design-day supply shortfalls are forecast to occur over the ten-year forecast; however, 
there is a design-hour shortfall beginning in the winter of 2027/28 based on the demand 
forecast filed in this rate filing.  Impacts to the East Gate supply generally will affect the 
Capital Region and may manifest at one or more Gate Stations, depending on the scenario.  
Impacts to the Troy Gate Station generally will affect customers in Troy and the 
immediately surrounding area.  Because there are no forecast supply shortfalls in the next 
several years, the Company has not evaluated the need for, or impact of, a potential 
moratorium on new service connections (and would consider alternative supply and 
demand solutions in the first place). 
3. 
Please see the chart below for a ten-year supply/demand design-day forecast for core 
customers based on the gas load forecast filed in the Gas Supply Panel’s direct testimony. 
Appendix A 
Page 19 of 33


--- Page 44 ---
Form 103  
As shown above, there is no design-day shortfall over the ten-year forecast, and thus the 
Company has no major capital projects planned to meet forecast demand.  The Albany 
Loop Project does not provide incremental supplies to the system.  It does, however, 
increase the Company’s ability to take in supplies from the Tennessee Gas Pipeline 
(“TGP”).  If incremental firm supplies are required from TGP in the future, the Company 
would need to pursue a contract with TGP separately, and any associated pipeline 
modifications would need to be completed under a separate project.

7. 
How did the Company determine the need for the requested two full-time equivalent

Will the requested FTE positions have job duties other than those needed for the

Response:  
 
1. 
Please refer to Attachments 1 and 2. 
 
2. 
The electric component for this program is under development and will be complete by 
the fourth quarter of fiscal year (“FY”) 2021.  
 
3. 
There is no data for calendar year (“CY”) 2017 or 2018 as the training commenced in 
CY2019. 
 
a. 
To date, drills have been conducted with 172 fire departments. 
 2019 – 49 departments 
 2020 – 123 departments 
 
b. 
To date, 3,090 firefighters and 255 trainers have been trained. 
 2019 – 1,748 firefighters in attendance and 81 trainers 
 2020 – 1,342 firefighters in attendance and 174 trainers 
 
c. 
The drills were attended by 18 paid fire departments, 154 volunteer fire 
departments, and 3 county hazmat departments.  
 
d. 
Drills were conducted at the respective fire and county hazmat departments. 
 
4. 
Training first responders directly affects, and will help to improve, public safety and the 
safety of first responders when responding to events related to natural gas and electric 
facilities.  Adequate training will also help to minimize the damage to Company facilities 
and system interruptions, where possible.  The intensive training curriculum and 
distribution of remote methane leak detection equipment is above and beyond the 
standard training provided and will help to foster a productive relationship with these 
entities that encourages open communications between the parties during times of 
emergency.  Additional benefits of the program include:  
 
 Additional training and education for first responders to help protect the communities 
they serve, which are home to our customers; 
 Provision of critical safety training that municipalities may not be able to fund due to 
budget restraints; 
Appendix A 
Page 23 of 33


--- Page 48 ---
Form 103  
 Provision of tools to fire departments that reduce response time to natural gas 
incidents;  
 The departments will be better equipped to notify the Company for quicker 
restoration; and 
 After training, a knowledgeable and educated resource within the department will be 
able to make a scene safe in the event of a natural gas leak. 
 
5. 
Because electric customers will also receive benefits from this program, the Company 
will change the allocation of program costs in its corrections and updates filing.  
  
6. 
The forecast costs are the costs for two new FTEs and associated expenses based on 
historical costs for the existing program.  The costs include the scope of work to 
accommodate the increasing demand for these programs, the need to re-visit existing first 
responder organizations, and to expand the program footprint into other communities and 
agencies within our service territory.  
 
7. 
The Company determined the need for two FTEs to be located in the Central Region to 
address the expanded footprint and larger geographical coverage required.  The FTEs will 
deliver first responder technical training programs through a blended learning approach 
such as hands on activities, train the trainer programs, and in-person training to simulate 
real tasks.  This approach ensures that the Company is effectively providing training 
aligned with external requirements, operational priorities, and best practice. 
 
8. 
As noted in the Company’s response to Question 7 above, the requested FTEs were based 
on additional work load associated with the First Responder Train-the-Trainer Program.  
Although the requested FTEs will have the ability to perform other periphery duties 
within the business based on their respective abilities (e.g., DOT training or CPR/First 
Aid Training), their primary responsibilities will be to deliver the first responder training 
program.

Smell Gas. 
Act Fast.
To report emergencies, call 911 
and National Grid immediately.

--- Page 50 ---
2
Learn how it behaves so you 
can respond safely.
• Natural gas is lighter than air and will 
move laterally or migrate upward when 
underground or in enclosed spaces.
• Natural gas has an explosive 
(flammable) concentration range 
between about 5% and 15% gas 
to air. At concentrations below 5% or 
above 15%, natural gas will not burn.
• Gas can accumulate in storm 
drains, construction trenches, 
buildings and other utility lines. 
As gas concentrations rise or fall, they 
can pass through the explosive range.
• Learn to recognize the common 
indicators of a leak:
	
– a distinctive, rotten-egg odor*
	
– a hissing, whistling or roaring sound
	
– dirt blowing into the air from a hole in 
the ground
	
– continuous bubbling in water
	
– dead or dying vegetation (in an 
otherwise moist area) over or near 
a pipeline
	
– a damaged connection to a gas 
appliance
	
– an exposed pipeline after a fire, flood 
or other disaster.
*The rotten-egg odor of natural gas comes from a 
chemical odorant called mercaptan. Be aware that 
some natural gas is not odorized, so some leaks 
may not be detectable by smell alone. Additionally, 
chemical or physical processes may strip the 
odorant from natural gas so that the gas no 
longer smells. This is known as “odor fade.”
Understanding the properties of natural gas
Don’t rely on your 
nose to assess a 
gas leak!
A fire crew 
responded to the 
report of a natural gas 
odor and conferred as 
they approached the 
doorway to the residence. 
They felt the gas did not smell 
“all that thick,” so they entered the
building without utilizing air-monitoring 
equipment. As they entered, a 
spark from a thermostat ignited the 
accumulated gas and caused an 
explosion that blew a door off its hinges 
and subjected the crew to an intense 
pressure wave. Once the presence of 
a gas odor was confirmed, the crew 
should have employed air-monitoring 
equipment and evacuated the premises.
Dead or dying 
vegetation
Dirt being thrown 
into the air
Water 
bubbling
Exposed pipeline

--- Page 51 ---
3
Take action to prevent natural 
gas fires.
A lit cigarette is enough to ignite 
leaking natural gas, and even the 
smallest electrical spark has been 
known to cause an explosion. If you 
suspect a gas leak, take these steps to 
minimize the possibility of ignition:
•	Use intrinsically safe radios 
and flashlights.
•	Do not ring 
doorbells or 
use garage 
door openers. 
Do not turn on or 
off any lights or 
electrical devices 
or appliances, as 
this could create 
a spark.
•	Do not step on doormats. 
Friction from boots could create static 
electricity.
•	Do not ventilate a building without 
first coordinating with National Grid 
or the local gas utility and verifying 
gas has been shut off and ignition 
sources have been eliminated.
•	Alert the local electric utility if the 
situation warrants a discontinuation of 
electric service to a building.
This booklet is designed to 
supplement – not replace – 
your department’s standard 
operating procedures for 
natural gas safety.
Be alert for 
ignition sources
Avoid potential sources 
of combustion
Common sources include doorbell and 
wall switches, static electricity, any type 
of electronic ignition, motors, vehicle 
engines, refrigerators, pilot lights, 
appliances, phones, pagers 
and flashlights.
Appendix A 
Page 27 of 33


--- Page 52 ---
4
Responding to gas leaks
Situational awareness 
saves lives
Responding to the report 
of a natural gas odor, a 
shift commander observed 
a backhoe operating at a 
residence under construction. 
Upon exiting his vehicle, 
he smelled a strong odor of 
natural gas and heard a loud 
hiss coming from a hole near 
the backhoe. He positioned 
all responding apparatus 
two houses away from the 
suspected leak and warned 
all workers to evacuate the 
site. As the backhoe operator 
reached the edge of the 
property, multiple floors of 
the home became engulfed in 
fire. Thanks to the appropriate 
actions of the shift commander, 
no injuries occurred during 
this incident.
• When you arrive at the scene, confirm your 
dispatcher has notified National Grid or 
the local gas utility.
• Park emergency vehicles away and upwind 
from the area, and safely out of the collapse 
zone of involved structures. Do not park over 
storm drains or manholes.
• Evacuate the area and nearby structures 
immediately – 330 feet in all directions, if possible. 
For larger leaks, consider downwind evacuation for 
at least a half-mile.
• Secure the perimeter and reroute traffic if necessary.
• Never enter a manhole, sewer or 
underground vault.
• Never enter a structure where gas is leaking 
unless it is safe to do so. If you must approach, 
wear full personal protective equipment (PPE) 
and self-contained breathing apparatus (SCBA).
• Monitor the atmosphere with your department’s 
approved and calibrated air-monitoring 
equipment (combustible gas indicator, 4-gas 
meter, etc.) which you have been trained to use.
Situational awareness 
is your key to safety.

--- Page 53 ---
5
Let the fire burn; protect 
exposures with a fog spray.
• If gas has ignited, let it burn. 
Extinguishing the fire may allow 
unburned gas to collect and cause 
an explosion.
• Do not use water to suppress a 
natural gas fire. It is ineffective and 
may introduce water into gas mains.
	
– You may use a fog spray to cool 
combustible exposures or gas valves 
and to assist with rescue operations.
• Evacuate to a distance of 330 feet 
in all directions; clear all buildings 
of occupants, and clear the area of 
bystanders.
• First responders who have been 
trained to do so may turn off gas 
at aboveground meter valves or 
appliance supply lines only – 
NEVER at underground pipeline 
valves or relief vents.
	
– Remain alert for gas migration and 
possible re-ignition.
	
– After a service valve or appliance 
supply line has been closed, 
do not open it under any 
circumstances. Only utility 
personnel can restore gas service.
	
– Inform National Grid or the local 
gas utility of any valve you have 
closed and its precise location. 
This information is critical for system 
safety and service restoration.
Responding to gas fires
© 2019 Culver Media, LLC
800-428-5837
Product #38200	 Run #6059    May 2019
Gas explosion occurs 
after structure fire is 
knocked down
While controlling a structure fire ignited 
by a lightning strike at a single-family 
residence, crews detected a natural gas 
odor. They quickly shut off gas service 
and notified command, who ordered an 
immediate evacuation. As firefighters 
exited the building, accumulated gas 
ignited and an explosion occurred, 
pushing fire out of the basement 
windows and dislodging an interior wall. 
Thanks to the timely gas shut-off and 
evacuation, no injuries were sustained.

Page 29 of 33


--- Page 54 ---
6
Make life safety your 
highest priority.
Natural gas explosions occur when 
leaking gas accumulates underground 
or in other enclosed areas and is ignited. 
A flammable range of approximately 5% 
to 15% is highly volatile, and any ignition 
source can cause an explosion.
• Exercise extreme caution when 
approaching and entering the scene.
• Clear the area as soon as possible 
after an explosion occurs to prevent loss 
of life. Evacuate 330 feet to a half-mile.
• Wear self-contained breathing 
apparatus (SCBA). 
• Be aware of the risk of secondary 
explosions. There could be additional 
gas trapped near the initial explosion 
site or in nearby buildings. Evacuate 
accordingly.
• In extreme or prolonged situations, 
contact the local electric utility 
to turn off power to the structures 
or area involved.
In case of a natural gas explosion
Prompt evacuation 
after gas pipe 
rupture
Construction 
workers damaged 
a gas pipeline, and 
the damage sent gas 
migrating into nearby 
buildings. Fire and gas 
company personnel quickly 
arrived on the scene and promptly 
evacuated the immediate area. Soon 
after, an explosion occurred, destroying 
one building and severely damaging 
adjacent structures. Three firefighters, 
two police officers and three other 
people were injured; thanks to speedy 
response and evacuation, no one 
was killed.

Page 30 of 33


--- Page 55 ---
7
A CO meter can detect what your 
senses cannot. 
Carbon monoxide (CO) is an odorless, 
tasteless and colorless toxic gas that 
accumulates rapidly and is difficult to 
detect. CO is created when combustion 
of any fuel occurs with insufficient oxygen. 
Victims can be exposed to harmful or even 
fatal levels of CO without realizing it.
If a CO incident is reported in a 
building served by natural gas, notify 
National Grid or the local gas utility 
and take these steps:
• Evacuate the building. Use full SCBA 
and PPE.
• Make sure victims seek fresh air and 
get immediate medical attention.
• Ventilate the building.
• Shut off the gas at the aboveground 
service valve before the meter, provided 
you have been trained to do so.
• Work with National Grid or the local 
gas utility to determine the cause of the 
carbon monoxide leak and when it is safe 
for occupants to return.
• Exercise extreme caution. CO is 
not only highly toxic, but also highly 
flammable. It will ignite when its 
concentration is 12 to 74 percent 
gas-in-air.
	
– If possible, carry a CO meter. It will 
help you identify the presence of CO 
when responding to medical calls 
and other situations where CO is 
not immediately suspected.
Carbon monoxide hazards
CO detectors save lives
The Consumer Product Safety Commission 
recommends installing one CO detector in 
the hallway near every separate sleeping 
area. Detectors must be installed away 
from areas of extreme temperature and 
humidity and at least 15 feet from fuel-
burning appliances.
Appendix A 
Page 31 of 33


--- Page 56 ---
Firefighters, police and EMTs are typically the first on the scene in an emergency and face the greatest risk 
from utility hazards. Understanding the potential dangers of electricity and natural gas and dealing with them 
correctly makes everyone safer:
• Immediately contact National Grid or the 
appropriate local utility when electric or 
natural gas lines are involved in an emergency.
• Park emergency vehicles away from the area 
of a utility-related incident.
• Keep yourself, your ladders and all aerial 
equipment at least 10 feet from all overhead 
power lines. Always use the maximum possible 
distance.
• Assume all power lines are energized as well 
as all objects and areas around electrical hazards.
• Never attempt to move fallen power lines or 
contact energized vehicles until utility personnel 
give the all clear. Secure the area; keep yourself 
and the public at least 30 feet away.
• Avoid and prevent ignition in the vicinity of 
natural gas leaks; evacuate the area.
• Turn off natural gas service at aboveground 
meter valves and appliance supply lines only. 
Never reopen a service valve or appliance supply 
line that has been closed.
• When natural gas is burning, let it burn and 
protect exposures.
• Make sure carbon monoxide victims get 
immediate medical attention.
First Responder Awareness
Visit firstresponder.
ngridsafety.com to 
earn Certificates of 
Completion in electric 
and natural gas 
utility safety.
